Compactly supported p p -adic pro-étale cohomology of analytic varieties Thanks: W. N.’s research was supported in part by the grant NR-19-CE40-0015-02 COLOSS, the NSF grant No. DMS-1440140, and the Simons Foundation. P.A. and W.N. were supported by the project KAPIBARA funded by the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No 802787). S.G.’s research was partially supported by the National Science Foundation under Grant No. DMS-1926686 and by the ERC under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No 804176).
Piotr Achinger, Sally Gilles, Wiesława Nizioł

Abstract
. We study properties of compactly supported p p -adic pro-étale cohomology of smooth partially proper rigid analytic varieties. In particular, we prove a comparison theorem, in a stable range, with compactly supported syntomic cohomology, which is built from compactly supported Hyodo-Kato and 𝐁 dR + {\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}} -cohomologies. We derive from that a (limited version of a) fundamental diagram. This paper should be thought of as a compactly supported counterpart of the series [ 15 ] , [ 16 ] , [ 17 ] but, in particular, as a continuation of [ 16 ] .



1.
Introduction

We wrote this paper to familiarize ourselves (and the reader) with the definition of compactly supported
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology of smooth partially proper rigid analytic varieties, to study its basic properties, and to present some computations. This paper should be thought of as a compactly supported counterpart of the series
[
15
]
,
[
16
]
,
[
17
]
but, in particular, as a continuation of
[
16
]
.

Let
K
be a complete discrete valuation field of characteristic 0 with a perfect
residue field
k
of characteristic
p
. Let
C
be the
p
-adic completion of an algebraic closure of
K
. Let
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
be the Galois group of
K
.
Let
F={\rm Frac}(W(k))
and
let
\varphi
be the absolute
Frobenius on
W(\overline{k})
.

1.1.
Definitions

The study of
p
-adic étale cohomology of analytic varieties over
C
is a relatively recent endeavour. It started in earnest with
[
32
]
, where Scholze proved, among other things, finiteness of
p
-adic étale cohomology of proper rigid analytic varieties. For compactly supported
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology of smooth partially proper varieties, it was not clear initially what the right definitions should be. There were two immediate guesses:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{(1)}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle:=({\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}/p^{n}))\otimes_{{\bf Z}_{p}}^{{\mathrm{L}}_{\Box}}{\bf Q}_{p};

(1.1)

\displaystyle\text{or }{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{(2)}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})

\displaystyle:=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p})],

\displaystyle\hphantom{aaa}\text{with }{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{p})=\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi_{X}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma(X\setminus Z,{\bf Q}_{p}),

where
\Phi_{X}
denotes the set of quasi-compact opens in
X
and the brackets
[...]
denote the mapping fiber. The first definition is just a continuous version of
{\bf Z}/p^{n}
-cohomology.
The heuristic for the second definition comes from the (possible) comparison with de Rham cohomology and the classical definition of van der Put of compactly supported version of the latter
[
36
]
. It came to us as a surprise that this definition agrees with Huber’s definition of compactly supported
p
-adic étale (sic !) cohomology (see Theorem
1.2
below): this follows from the canonical quasi-isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{(2)}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{p})_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{(2)}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

The fact that this might be the right definition comes from the work on dualities which pairs this cohomology with
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology (see
[
12
]
,
[
13
]
,
[
27
]
,
[
1
]
). Hence we set

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{(2)}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

We note that, if we take étale instead of pro-étale in the first definition of (
1.1
), we obtain an isomorphic object. The indication that this is actually a good definition of compactly supported
p
-adic étale cohomology (sic !) comes from two sources:

(1)

in
[
11
]
, it was shown that, for
p
-adic period domains, it behaves like
\ell
-adic cohomology for
\ell\neq p
;

(2)

in
[
26
]
, it was shown that, for smooth almost proper varieties (i.e.,
X=\overline{X}\setminus Z
, for a smooth proper variety
\overline{X}
and a closed Zariski subvariety
Z
), it satisfies Poincaré duality vis a vis
p
-adic étale cohomology.

Hence we set

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma^{(1)}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

It is a nonobvious fact but there exists a canonical map
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})
.

1.2.
Main results

The main result of the paper can be summed up in the following theorem-construction. We write
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
for the
\infty
-derived category
of solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-modules and
{\mathscr{F}}{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
for the corresponding filtered
\infty
-derived category.

Theorem 1.2
.

To any smooth partially proper rigid analytic variety
X
over
C
there are naturally associated:

(1)

The pro-étale cohomology with compact support
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
. There is a natural quasi-isomorphism
in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
with Huber’s étale cohomology with compact support

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}).

(1.3)

(2)

The syntomic cohomology with compact support
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{syn},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
,
r\in{\bf N}
, with a natural period morphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})

\alpha_{r}:{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{syn},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r)),

(1.4)

which is a quasi-isomorphism after truncation
\tau_{\leq r}
.

(3)

The de Rham cohomology
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X)
and
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-cohomology
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})
. These are filtered dg
C
- and
{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}
-algebras, respectively,
such that in
{\mathscr{F}}{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})\otimes^{{\mathrm{L}}_{\Box}}_{{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}}C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X).

(4)

The Hyodo-Kato cohomology

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X)
. This is a dg
F^{\operatorname{nr}}
-algebra
1
1

1

We define
F^{\rm nr}
as the maximal unramified extension of
F
in
\overline{K}
.

equipped with a Frobenius
\varphi
and a monodromy operator
N
. We have :

(a)

Natural Hyodo-Kato quasi-isomorphisms in
{\mathscr{D}}(C_{\Box})
and
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR},\Box})
, respectively:

\displaystyle\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}

\displaystyle:{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X){\otimes}^{{\mathrm{L}}_{\Box}}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X),

\displaystyle\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}

\displaystyle:{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X){\otimes}^{{\mathrm{L}}_{\Box}}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}}).

(b)

(Poincaré duality)
If
X
is everywhere of dimension
d
then the natural pairing of solid
(\varphi,N)
-modules over
F^{\rm nr}

H^{i}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\otimes^{\Box}_{F_{L}}H^{2d-i}_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cup}}{{\to}}H^{2d}_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X)\xrightarrow{{\rm tr}_{\operatorname{HK}}}F^{\rm nr}\{-d\}

is a perfect duality, i.e., we have induced isomorphisms of solid
(\varphi,N)
-modules over
F^{\rm nr}

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm{Hom}}_{F^{\rm nr}_{\Box}}(H^{2d-i}_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X),F^{\rm nr}\{-d\}),

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm{Hom}}_{F^{\rm nr}_{\Box}}(H^{2d-i}_{\operatorname{HK}}(X),F^{\rm nr}\{-d\}).

These duality isomorphisms are compatible, via the Hyodo-Kato isomorphism, with the de Rham duality.

(5)

A distinguished triangle in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{syn},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits}[{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X){\otimes}^{{\mathrm{L}}_{\Box}}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}]^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}}}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{r},

(1.5)

which can be lifted to the derived category of Topological Vector Spaces.

(6)

(Trace maps)
If
X
is of dimension
d
, then there is a natural trace map

{\rm tr}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},X}:H^{2d}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(d))\to{\bf Q}_{p},

which is compatible with both Huber’s trace map and the syntomic trace map (via (
1.3
) and (
1.4
), respectively).

Some of the constructions in the above theorem follow in a rather straightforward, if technically tedious
2
2

2

Tedious especially in the functional analytic part.
, manner from the ones for usual cohomologies. Others, like the comparison with the Huber’s definition, the definition of the trace maps, the Poincaré duality for Hyodo-Kato cohomology, and the related computations require subtler arguments. The duality for Hyodo-Kato cohomology is induced from the one for de Rham cohomology (a classical result) but the information about Frobenius and monodromy (as well as Galois action if needed) is obtained by a reduction (via excision) to the proper case. The pro-étale trace map is defined by passing to the syntomic cohomology via period quasi-isomorphism and then using the Hyodo-Kato trace map (via the distinguished triangle (
1.5
)). That it agrees with Huber’s trace map can be checked by a reduction to the algebraic case (again, via excision) where this amounts to the compatibility of the algebraic
p
-adic comparison morphism with traces.

From Theorem
1.2
, we derive the following key result:

Corollary 1.6
.

Let
X
be a smooth partially proper rigid analytic variety over
C
.

(1)

(Basic comparison theorem)

Let
r\geq 0
. There is a natural quasi-isomorphism
(period isomorphism)
in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
:

\displaystyle\tau_{\leq r}

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\simeq\tau_{\leq r}\big[[{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{HK}}(X){\otimes}^{{\mathrm{L}}_{\Box}}_{F^{\operatorname{nr}}}{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}]^{N=0,\varphi=p^{r}}\smash{\mathop{\relbar\joinrel\longrightarrow}\limits^{\iota_{\operatorname{HK}}}}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{dR}}(X/{\bf B}^{+}_{\operatorname{dR}})/F^{r}\big].

(1.7)

(2)

(Fundamental exact sequence)
If
X
is Stein of dimension
d
over
K
and
r\geq 0
, then, under an assumption on the slopes of Frobenius on Hyodo-Kato cohomology (see Theorem
8.4
for details), there is a map of exact sequences of solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-modules

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{c}^{d}(X_{C},\Omega^{r-d-1})/\operatorname{Ker}d(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{\operatorname{syn},c}^{r}(X_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(H^{r}_{\operatorname{HK},c}(X_{C})\otimes^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\square$}}_{F^{\rm nr}}t^{d}\operatorname{{\mathbf{B}}^{+}_{\operatorname{st}}})^{\varphi=p^{r},N=0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{c}^{d}(X_{C},\Omega^{r-d-1})/\operatorname{Ker}d(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{c}^{d}(X_{C},\Omega^{r-d})^{d=0}(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{r}_{\operatorname{dR},c}(X_{C})(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

The Frobenius slope condition above is very restrictive but it holds for tori and Drinfeld spaces, for example (probably also for general
p
-adic period domains) so we can use the fundamental diagram to compute compactly supported cohomology in these cases. Recall that for the usual cohomology we have a fundamental diagram with no restrictions on the slopes
3
3

3

This means that the authors of
[
9
]
got very lucky that they started computations with the usual cohomology and not with the compactly supported one as is customary in the local Langlands program.
.

Finally, the paper includes a number of concrete examples of computations of compactly supported
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology: Drinfeld spaces and tori (already mentioned above) but also affine spaces, Stein curves, and general Stein spaces (the last one – up to a degree).

Remark 1.8
.

(1)

We also prove analogs of Theorem
1.2
and Corollary
1.6
for smooth overconvergent varieties and compare them with their rigid analytic versions as in
[
16
]
. Here, again, it was not clear what should be the right definition of compactly supported
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology of an overconvergent variety and our definition is inspired, as in the rigid analytic case, by the definition of compactly supported de Rham cohomology of dagger affinoids of Grosse-Klönne from
[
21
]
.

(2)

For a further study of properties of compactly supported
p
-adic cohomologies of rigid analytic spaces see the work of Xinhyu Shao
[
34
]
. For a further study of fundamental
diagrams and exact sequences in the same context see the work of Sally Gilles
[
20
]
.

Notation and conventions.

Let
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
be a complete discrete valuation ring with fraction field
K
of characteristic 0 and with perfect
residue field
k
of characteristic
p
. Let
\overline{K}
be an algebraic closure of
K
and let
{\mathscr{O}}_{\overline{K}}
denote the integral closure of
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
in
\overline{K}
. Let
C=\widehat{\overline{K}}
be the
p
-adic completion of
\overline{K}
. Let

W(k)
be the ring of Witt vectors of
k
with
fraction field
F
(i.e.,
W(k)={\mathscr{O}}_{F}
); let
e=e_{K}
be the ramification index of
K
over
F
. Set
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}=\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{K}/K)
and
let
\varphi
be the absolute
Frobenius on
W(\overline{k})
.
We will denote by
{\bf A}_{\operatorname{cr}},{\bf B}_{\operatorname{cr}},{\bf B}_{\operatorname{st}},{\bf B}_{\operatorname{dR}}
the crystalline, semistable, and de Rham period rings of Fontaine.

We will denote by
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
,

{\mathscr{O}}_{K}^{\times}
, and
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}^{0}
, depending on the context, the scheme
\operatorname{Spec}({{\mathscr{O}}_{K}})
or the formal scheme
\operatorname{Spf}({\mathscr{O}}_{K})
with the trivial, the canonical (i.e., associated to the closed point), and the induced by
{\bf N}\to{\mathscr{O}}_{K},1\mapsto 0
,
log-structure, respectively. Unless otherwise stated all formal schemes are
p
-adic, locally of finite type, and equidimensional. For a (
p
-adic formal) scheme
X
over
{\mathscr{O}}_{K}
, let

X_{0}
denote
the special fiber of
X
; let
X_{n}
denote its reduction modulo
p^{n+1}
.

All rigid analytic spaces considered will be over
K
or
C
.
We assume that they are separated, taut
4
4

4

That is, for all quasi-compact opens

V
of
X
, the closure
\overline{V}
of
V
in
X
is quasi-compact.
, and countable at infinity.

L=K,C
, we let
{\rm Sm}_{L}
(resp.

L
, and

Unless otherwise stated, we work in the derived (stable)
\infty
-category

{\mathscr{D}}(A)
of left-bounded complexes of a quasi-abelian category
A
(the latter will be clear from the context).
Many of our constructions will involve (pre)sheaves of objects from

.

For functional analysis, we will work in the context of condensed mathematics. We write
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{p,\Box})
for the
\infty
-derived category of solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-modules; (a bit abusively) we will write
{\bf Q}_{p,\Box}
for the category of solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-modules. We will use similar notation for modules over a solid
{\bf Q}_{p}
-ring etc.

2.
Pro-étale cohomology with compact support

Let
\ell
be a prime number. In this chapter, we define compactly supported
\ell
-adic pro-étale cohomology for partially proper smooth rigid analytic varieties and smooth dagger varieties over
K
or
C
. This cohomology was used in
[
12
]
to obtain a Poincaré duality for arithmetic
p
-adic pro-étale cohomology of rigid analytic Stein curves.

2.1.
Rigid analytic compact support functor

We start with a preliminary discussion.
Let
L=K,C
and let
X
be a smooth rigid analytic variety over
L
. Let
\Phi
denote the set of quasi-compact opens of
X
. We write
\partial X
for the
pro-object
\{X\setminus Z\}_{Z\in\Phi}
indexed by
\Phi^{\rm op}
. We note that
X\setminus Z
is an admissible open of
X
(see
[
36
, Lemma 1.5]
). For a presheaf
F
on smooth rigid analytic varieties with values in an
\infty
-category
{\mathscr{C}}
admitting filtered colimits, we set

F(\partial X):=\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}F(X\setminus Z).

We have a natural restriction map
F(X)\to F(\partial X)
. Suppose that
{\mathscr{C}}
is stable and set

F_{c}(X):=[F(X)\to F(\partial X)],

so that we have a distinguished triangle

F_{c}(X)\to F(X)\to F(\partial X).

Note that if
X
is quasi-compact then
\Phi
admits a final object
X
and if, moreover,
F(\emptyset)
is the final object, then we have

F_{c}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}F(X)
.

Suppose now that
F
is a sheaf. Let
U\subset X
be an open. For every
Z\in\Phi
whose closure is contained in
U
, i.e.,
Z\subset\subset_{X}U
, covering
X
by
X\setminus Z
and
U
, we have a cartesian square

\textstyle{F(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(X\setminus Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(U\setminus Z)}

and hence, setting
F_{Z}(-):=[F(-)\to F(-\setminus Z)]
, we get the excision

F_{Z}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}F_{Z}(U).

(2.1)

Now suppose that
j:U\to X
is partially proper, so that for every quasi-compact open
Z
of
U
, the closure of
Z
in
X
is also contained in
U
. Letting
Z
run over
\Phi_{U}
(quasi-compact opens for
U
), we get the pushforward map

j_{*}:F_{c}(U)\to F_{c}(X)

as the composition:

F_{c}(U)=\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi_{U}}F_{Z}(U)\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi_{U}}F_{Z}(X)\to\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}F_{Z}(X)=F_{c}(X).

We used here (
2.1
). Clearly, the map
j_{*}
is functorial in
F
.

2.2.
Rigid analytic setting

Following the same process as in Section
2.1
, we define compactly supported (pro-)étale cohomology of a smooth rigid analytic variety
X
over
K
or
C
. We give the definition for torsion coefficients as well as
\ell
-adic ones. We show that our construction coincides with the one of Huber (
[
23
]
) in the case of partially proper varieties.

2.2.1.
Definition

Let
L=K,C
. Let
X
be a smooth rigid analytic variety over
L
. We define compactly supported cohomology with torsion coefficients of
X
by the same formulas as in Section
2.1
, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*,c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})

\displaystyle:=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*}(\partial X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})],

where
*=\operatorname{\acute{e}t},\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}
and

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*}(\partial X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*}(X\setminus Z,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z}).

We then define the integral cohomologies by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})],

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:={\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z}).

We obtain complexes in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Z}_{\ell,\Box})
.

Remark 2.2
.

If we define

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell}):=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})],

the natural map

\operatorname{colim}_{Z}{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus Z,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})\to{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}\operatorname{colim}_{Z}\\
R\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus Z,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})

induces a map

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})
. In general, it is not a quasi-isomorphism.

For the rational theory, we set:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\otimes^{{\mathrm{L}}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\square$}}}_{{\bf Z}_{\ell}}{\bf Q}_{\ell},

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})

\displaystyle:=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})].

These are complexes in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})
.

Lemma 2.3
.

(1)

The natural morphism
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})
is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Z}_{\ell,\Box})
.

(2)

It induces a natural change of topology morphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Z}_{\ell,\Box})
:

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell}).

(2.4)

Remark 2.5
.

If
X
is partially proper, we will show below (see Theorem
2.23
) that the natural morphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\otimes_{{\bf Z}_{\ell}}^{{\mathrm{L}}_{\Box}}{\bf Q}_{\ell}\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})

is a quasi-isomorphism. It follows that the map (
2.4
) induces a natural morphism
in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})
:

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}).

Proof.

We first show that we have a quasi-isomorphism:

f_{Y}:{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Z}_{\ell}),

(2.6)

for any smooth rigid analytic variety
Y
. For that, choose a covering of
Y
by an increasing sequence of quasi-compact opens
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in{\bf N}}
. Since

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*}(Y,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{i}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*}(U_{i},{\bf Z}_{\ell}),

it suffices to show the quasi-isomorphism (
2.6
) for a quasi-compact
Y
.

Assume thus that
Y
is quasi-compact. Then we have

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Z}_{\ell})={\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})

using
[
32
, (3.17)]
. Since the functors
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma
and
{\mathrm{R}}\lim
commute, we have

{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})\xleftarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(Y,{\bf Z}_{\ell}),

where the last quasi-isomorphism follows from
[
32
, (8.2)]
. Hence we get the quasi-isomorphism (
2.6
), as wanted.

By the definitions of
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})
and
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})
, this induces a quasi-isomorphism:

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\xleftarrow[f]{\sim}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})

and composing with the natural morphism
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})
, we obtain the morphism from the second claim of the lemma.
∎

The above cohomologies satisfy all the properties from Section
2.1
, i.e., for
\Lambda\in\{{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z},{\bf Z}_{\ell},{\bf Q}_{\ell}\}
, we have (here
*=\operatorname{\acute{e}t},\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}
):

(1)

We have the distinguished triangles:

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*,c}(X,\Lambda)\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*}(X,\Lambda)\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*}(\partial X,\Lambda).

(2)

If
X
is quasi-compact, we have quasi-isomorphisms:

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*,c}(X,\Lambda)\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*}(X,\Lambda).

In particular, if
X
is proper its cohomology with compact support coincides with the usual cohomology.

(3)

Let
j:U\hookrightarrow X
is a relatively partially proper open subspace of
X
, we have a canonical map

j_{*}:\quad{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*,c}(U,\Lambda)\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{*,c}(X,\Lambda).

Remark 2.7
.

As we have seen above,
if
X
is quasi-compact, the cohomology of the boundary of
X
is trivial, i.e.,

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,\Lambda)=0
. Hence, in this case, our definition is not the correct one. For torsion coefficients, the étale cohomology with compact support of Huber
H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})
gives the right groups. For example,
if
\ell\neq p
and
X=\operatorname{Sp}(C\{T\})
is the closed unit ball, we have (see
[
23
, Ex 0.4.6]
) :

H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})\simeq\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }i\neq 2,\\
{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}&\text{ if }i=2.\end{cases}

2.2.2.
Comparison with the cohomology of Huber: integral coefficients

Compactly supported étale cohomology was previously defined by Huber in
[
22
]
,
[
23
]
. The purpose of this section is to prove the following comparison theorem.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
L=K,C
and let
\ell
be a prime number. Assume that
X
is partially proper over
L
. Then there exist natural quasi-isomorphisms in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Z}_{\ell,\Box})

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell}).

Let us start with reviewing the definition of cohomology with compact support of Huber from
[
22
, Ch. 5]
(and extended to the
\ell
-adic case in
[
23
]
).

(i)
Torsion coefficients.

Let
A
be a ring and let
f:X\to Y
be a separated morphism of separated rigid analytic varieties.

Definition 2.9
.

For
\mathscr{F}
, a sheaf of
\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-A)
, we define
\Gamma_{c}(X/Y,\mathscr{F})
in the following way:

(1)

If
X
and
Y
are qcqs, we choose an integral model
\tilde{f}:\mathfrak{X}\to\mathfrak{Y}
of
f
and set:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(X/Y,\mathscr{F}):=\{s\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})\;|\;\text{ there exists closed }\mathfrak{Z}\subset\mathfrak{X}\text{ such that }\mathfrak{Z}\to\mathfrak{Y}\text{ is proper }

\displaystyle\text{ and }s|_{{\rm sp}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}\setminus\mathfrak{Z})}=0\},

where
{\rm sp}:X\to\mathfrak{X}
is the specialization morphism.

(2)

If
Y
is qcqs, we set:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(X/Y,\mathscr{F}):=\{s\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})\;|

\displaystyle\;\text{there exists }\{U,V\},\text{ an admissible open covering of }X,

\displaystyle\text{ such that }U\text{ is quasi-compact, }s|_{U}\in\Gamma_{c}(U/Y,\mathscr{F}|_{U})\text{ and, }s_{V}=0\}.

(3)

In the general case, we set:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(X/Y,\mathscr{F}):=\{s\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{F})\;|

\displaystyle\;\text{for all }W\subset Y\text{ admissible open qcqs,}

\displaystyle s|_{f^{-1}(W)}\in\Gamma_{c}(f^{-1}(W)/W,\mathscr{F}|_{f^{-1}(W)})\}.

If
X
is a rigid analytic variety over
C
, we write simply
\Gamma_{c}(X,\mathscr{F})
pour
\Gamma_{c}(X/\operatorname{Sp}(C),\mathscr{F})
.
Huber defined the functor
f_{!}:\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-A)\to\operatorname{mod}(Y_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-A)
by setting

f_{!}\mathscr{F}(U):=\Gamma_{c}(X\times_{Y}U/U,\mathscr{F}),

for an étale open
U
of
Y
(see
[
22
, (5.6.2)]
).

Remark 2.10
.

If
X\to Y
is a morphism of adic spaces, we write
\Gamma_{c}(X/Y,\mathscr{F})
for the set of global sections of
\mathscr{F}
whose support is proper over
Y
.
If now
X\to Y
is a morphism of rigid analytic varieties as above and
r(X)\to r(Y)
is the morphism of associated adic spaces, we have an isomorphism (see
[
22
, (5.6.4)]
) :

\Gamma_{c}(X/Y,\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma_{c}(r(X)/r(Y),\rho^{*}\mathscr{F}),

where
\rho
is the natural morphism
r(X)_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}\to X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
.

(ii)
\ell
-adic coefficients.

Denote by
\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet})
the category of projective systems
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}
of
{\bf Z}_{\ell}
-modules on
X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
such that, for all
n\in{\bf N}
,
\ell^{n}\mathscr{F}_{n}=0
. Consider
X\to Y:=\operatorname{Spa}(L,{\mathscr{O}}_{L})
, a separated adic space locally of finite type
5
5

5

In fact, Huber gives a definition for more general morphisms
X\to Y
, which he calls
locally of
+
weakly finite type.
and assume that
X
is taut. If
X
is partially proper, we define
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,-)
as the right derived functor
of the functor

(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}\mapsto\Gamma_{c}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{F}_{n}):\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet})\to\operatorname{mod}({\bf Z}_{\ell}).

In the general case, there exists a factorization

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\overline{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y}

where
i
is an open immersion and
\overline{X}\to Y
is partially proper. We then define

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(\overline{X},(i_{!}\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}),

where
i_{!}{\mathscr{G}}
is the functor
U\mapsto\Gamma_{c}(X\times_{Y}U,{\mathscr{G}})
.

For
X
a separated rigid analytic variety over
C
, we define
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(r(X),(\rho^{*}\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n})
. If
X
is partially proper,
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,-)
is equal to the right derived functor of the functor
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}\mapsto\Gamma_{c}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\varprojlim_{n}\mathscr{F}_{n})
.

We will need the following well-known technical result:

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}\in\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet})
. There exists a monomorphism
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}\hookrightarrow(\mathscr{I}_{n})_{n}
from
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}
to
(\mathscr{I}_{n})_{n}
, an object of
\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet})
of the form

\mathscr{I}_{n}=\prod_{s=1}^{n}\mathscr{J}_{s},

where the maps
\mathscr{I}_{n}\to\mathscr{I}_{n-1}
are given by the canonical projections and
\mathscr{J}_{s}
are injective sheaves of
{\bf Z}/\ell^{s}
-modules on
X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
.

Moreover, for all
n
, the sheaf
\mathscr{I}_{n}
is an injective object of
\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})
and the canonical map
\mathscr{F}_{n}\to\mathscr{I}_{n}
is a monomorphism.

Proof.

We will recall here the proof from
[
24
, (1.1)]
. For all
s
, we choose a monomorphism
\mathscr{F}_{s}\to\mathscr{J}_{s}
of
\mathscr{F}_{s}
into an injective object of the category of sheaves of
{\bf Z}/\ell^{s}
-modules and we define the projective system

(\mathscr{I}_{n})_{n}
as in the statement of the lemma. Since the
\mathscr{J}_{s}
’s are injective,
(\mathscr{J}_{s})_{s\in{\bf N}}
is an injective object of the product category

C_{\ell}:=\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}/\ell^{s})

and
(\mathscr{F}_{s})_{s}\to(\mathscr{J}_{s})_{s}
is a monomorphism in that category. The functor

P:C_{\ell}\to\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet}),\quad(\mathscr{A}_{s})_{s}\mapsto(\prod_{s=1}^{n}\mathscr{A}_{s})_{n}

admits left adjoint given by the forgetful functor

V:\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet})\to C_{\ell}.

Since
V
is exact and faithful, the functor
P
preserves monomorphisms and injective objects. It follows that
(\mathscr{I}_{n})_{n}:=P((\mathscr{J}_{s})_{s})
is an injective object and we have a monomorphism
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}\to(\mathscr{I}_{n})_{n}
.

Conversely, for all
m\in{\bf N}
, the functor

V_{m}:\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet})\to\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}/\ell^{m}),\quad(\mathscr{A}_{n})_{n}\mapsto\mathscr{A}_{m}

also admits an exact faithful left adjoint:

U_{m}:\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}/\ell^{m})\to\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet}),\text{ where }U_{m}((\mathscr{A}_{n})_{n})=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }n<m,\\
\mathscr{A}_{m}&\text{ if }n\geq m.\end{cases}

It follows that
V_{n}
preserves monomorphisms and injectives.
Hence, we have obtained that
\mathscr{F}_{n}\to\mathscr{I}_{n}=V_{n}((\mathscr{I}_{m})_{m})
is a monomorphism of
\mathscr{F}_{n}
into an injective sheaf
\mathscr{I}_{n}
, as wanted.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

This lemma allows us to construct injective resolutions
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}\to(\mathscr{I}_{n})^{\bullet}_{n}
such that the induced sequences
\mathscr{F}_{n}\to\mathscr{I}_{n}^{\bullet}
are also injective resolutions.

We can now prove the following proposition:

Proposition 2.13
.

(Huber,
[
23
, 2.3]
)

There exists an isomorphism of exact functors from
D^{+}(\operatorname{mod}(X_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet}))
to
D^{+}(\operatorname{mod}({\bf Z}_{\ell}))
:

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{!}\circ{\mathrm{R}}\pi_{*},

where
\pi_{*}
is the discretization functor

\pi_{*}:\operatorname{mod}(X_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet})\to\operatorname{mod}(X_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}),\quad({\mathscr{F}}_{n})_{n}\mapsto\lim_{n}{\mathscr{F}}_{n}

and
\Gamma_{!}
is the functor

\Gamma_{!}:\operatorname{mod}(X_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to\operatorname{mod}({\bf Z}_{\ell}),\quad\mathscr{F}\mapsto\Gamma_{c}(X_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}},\mathscr{F}).

Proof.

By definition,
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,-)
is equal to the functor
{\mathrm{R}}(\Gamma_{c}\circ\pi_{*})
. Take a resolution
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}\to(\mathscr{I}_{n})^{\bullet}_{n}
given by Remark
2.12
.
The functor
\pi_{*}
is given by
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}\mapsto\lim_{n}\mathscr{F}_{n}
, hence we have

\pi_{*}((\mathscr{I}^{\bullet}_{n})_{n})=\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{s}.

This yields

{\mathrm{R}}(\Gamma_{c}\circ\pi_{*})(({\mathscr{F}}_{n})_{n})\simeq\Gamma_{c}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{s}).

But, by
[
22
, (5.2.6)]
, every term of the complex
\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{n}
is
\Gamma_{!}
-acyclic, hence we have

({\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{!}\circ{\mathrm{R}}\pi_{*})(({\mathscr{F}}_{n})_{n})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{!}(\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{s})\simeq\Gamma_{!}(\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{s})=\Gamma_{c}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{s}).

We have obtained
that
{\mathrm{R}}(\Gamma_{!}\circ\pi_{*})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{!}\circ{\mathrm{R}}\pi_{*}
, as wanted.
∎

Later on we will need the following lemma:

Lemma 2.14
.

For
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}
in
\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell}^{\bullet})
, there exists a quasi-isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\mathrm{R}}\pi_{*}(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathscr{F}_{n}).

Proof.

As in the proof of the previous proposition, take an injective resolution
(\mathscr{F}_{n})_{n}\to(\mathscr{I}_{n})^{\bullet}_{n}
given by Remark
2.12
. We have
\pi_{*}((\mathscr{I}_{n})^{\bullet}_{n})=\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{s}
. But each term of the complex
\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{s}
is
\Gamma
-acyclic and
\Gamma
commutes with countable products, hence we get:

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\mathrm{R}}\pi_{*}(\mathscr{F}_{n}))\simeq\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{s}).

Remark
2.12
also gives us, for all
n
, an injective resolution
\mathscr{F}_{n}\to\mathscr{I}^{\bullet}_{n}
of
\mathscr{F}_{n}
. Hence

{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathscr{F}_{n})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathscr{I}^{\bullet}_{n})\simeq\prod_{s\in{\bf N}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathscr{J}^{\bullet}_{s}),

which proves our lemma.
∎

(iii)
Comparison theorem.

Let
L=K,C
.
Assume in this section that
X
is partially proper over
L
. For a quasi-compact open
Z
and a sheaf of
{\bf Z}_{\ell}
-module
\mathscr{F}
on
X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, define

\Gamma_{Z}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F}):=\operatorname{Ker}(\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F})\to\Gamma((X\setminus Z)_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F}))

and
H^{i}_{Z}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F}):=H^{i}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{Z}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F})
the associated cohomology.

Lemma 2.15
.

(Huber,
[
22
, Prop. 5.6.5]
)

For all
\mathscr{F}
in
\operatorname{mod}(X_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell})
, we have

\Gamma_{c}(X_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}},\mathscr{F})=\{s\in\Gamma(X_{\emph{\'{e}t}},\mathscr{F})\;|\;\exists Z\subset X\text{ an admissible quasi-compact open such that }s|_{X\setminus Z}=0\}.

In particular
\Gamma_{c}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F})=\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}\Gamma_{Z}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F})
.

Proof.

If
s
is in
\Gamma_{c}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F})
then, by definition (see
2.9
), there exists an admissible open covering
(Z,V)
, with
Z
quasi-compact open, such that
s|_{V}=0
. Since
V\supset X\setminus Z
, we have

s|_{X\setminus Z}=0
as well.

Conversely, let
s
be such that
s|_{X\setminus Z}=0
, for
Z
admissible open and quasi-compact. Let
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
be an admissible covering of
X
with
X_{i}
open affinoid and such that, for all
i
, there exists
\tilde{X}_{i}
an open affinoid
of
X
with
X_{i}\Subset\tilde{X}_{i}
. Since
Z
is quasi-compact, modulo reindexing, we may assume that
Z\subset X_{1}\cup X_{2}\cup\dots\cup X_{n}
, for a constant
n
. Set
V:=X\setminus(X_{1}\cup\dots\cup X_{n})
and
\tilde{V}:=\tilde{X}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\tilde{X}_{n}
. Then
\{V,\tilde{V}\}
is an admissible open covering of
X
and
s|_{V}=0
.

Take a formal model
\tilde{\mathfrak{V}}
of
\tilde{V}
and the opens
\mathfrak{X}_{i}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{i}
of
\tilde{\mathfrak{V}}
with
\mathfrak{X}_{i}\subset\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{i}
and
X_{i}={\rm sp}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})
,
\tilde{X}_{i}={\rm sp}^{-1}(\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{i})

(where
{\rm sp}:\tilde{V}\to\tilde{\mathfrak{V}}
is the specialization map). If we write
\bar{\mathfrak{X}}_{i}
for the closure of
\mathfrak{X}_{i}
in
\tilde{\mathfrak{X}}_{i}
then
\bar{\mathfrak{X}}_{i}
is proper over
{\mathscr{O}}_{L}
(by
[
29
, 2.5]
). Let now
\mathfrak{Z}:=\bar{\mathfrak{X}}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\bar{\mathfrak{X}}_{n}
. Then
\mathfrak{Z}
is proper over
{\mathscr{O}}_{L}
and
{\rm sp}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}\setminus\mathfrak{Z})\subset(X\setminus Z)
hence
s|_{{\rm sp}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}\setminus\mathfrak{Z})}=0
. We have proved that
s\in\Gamma_{c}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F})
.
∎

Denote by
F
the functor

F:\operatorname{mod}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to\operatorname{mod}({\bf Z}_{\ell}),\quad F(\mathscr{F}):=\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}\Gamma_{Z}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F}).

Proposition 2.16
.

(van der Put,
[
36
, (1.3)]
)

For all sheaves
\mathscr{F}
of
\operatorname{mod}(X_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell})
, there exists a natural quasi-isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}F(\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\sim}[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X,\mathscr{F})\to\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}}(X\setminus Z,\mathscr{F})].

Proof.

It suffices to check that, for all quasi-compact opens

Z
, we have a distinguished triangle:

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{Z}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathscr{F})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus Z,\mathscr{F}),

where
{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{Z}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},-)
is the right derived functor of
\mathscr{F}\mapsto\Gamma_{Z}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F})
.

To prove that, take the injective resolution
\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{I}^{\bullet}(\mathscr{F})
defined as follows. For
n=0
, we set
\mathscr{I}^{0}(\mathscr{F})(U):=\prod_{x\in U}\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
, where the product is taken over the geometric points
x
of
X
such that
x\in U
and
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}_{x}
is the injective envelope of
\mathscr{F}_{x}
. We define then by induction
\mathscr{I}^{n+1}(\mathscr{F}):=\mathscr{I}^{n}(\mathscr{I}_{n}/\mathscr{F})
. We have that
\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathscr{I}^{n}({\mathscr{F}}))\to\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus Z,\mathscr{I}^{n}({\mathscr{F}}))
is surjective, for all
n\geq 0
.
It follows that the sequence of complexes

0\to\Gamma_{Z}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{I}^{\bullet}({\mathscr{F}}))\to\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,\mathscr{I}^{\bullet}({\mathscr{F}}))\to\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus Z,\mathscr{I}^{\bullet}({\mathscr{F}}))\to 0

is exact, as wanted.
∎

Corollary 2.17
.

For all sheaves
\mathscr{F}\in\operatorname{mod}(X_{\emph{\text{\'{e}t}}}-{\bf Z}_{\ell})
, we have a natural quasi-isomorphism

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{!}(X,\mathscr{F})\xrightarrow{\sim}[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\text{\emph{\'{e}t}}}(X,\mathscr{F})\to\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus Z,\mathscr{F})].

Proof.

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{!}(X,-)
is the right derived functor of the functor
\mathscr{F}\mapsto\Gamma_{c}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathscr{F})
and hence it is equal to
{\mathrm{R}}F
by Lemma
2.15
. The result follows now directly from Proposition
2.16
.
∎

Proof.

(
of Theorem
2.8
) We want to show that

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell}).

For that, consider the following quasi-isomorphisms:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{!}(X,{\mathrm{R}}\pi_{*}({\bf Z}/\ell^{n})_{n})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\mathrm{R}}\pi_{*}({\bf Z}/\ell^{n})_{n})\to\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus Z,{\mathrm{R}}\pi_{*}({\bf Z}/\ell^{n})_{n})]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}[{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})\to\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}{\mathrm{R}}\lim_{n}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus Z,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})]

\displaystyle=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to\operatorname{colim}_{Z\in\Phi}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus Z,{\bf Z}_{\ell})].

The first and second quasi-isomorphisms
follow from Proposition
2.13
and Corollary
2.17
, respectively. The third one follows from Lemma
2.14
. The equality in the fourth row is obtained from the definition of
\ell
-adic étale cohomology.
∎

Corollary 2.18
.

Assume that
\ell\neq p
. Then the cohomology groups
H_{\text{\emph{\'{e}t}},c}^{i}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})
are finitely generated
{\bf Z}/{\ell^{n}}
-modules and we have an isomorphism

H^{i}_{\text{\emph{\'{e}t}},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{\sim}\varprojlim_{n}H^{i}_{\text{\emph{\'{e}t}},c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}).

Proof.

The first claim follows from Theorem
2.8
and the fact that we know the result for cohomology groups with compact support of Huber
H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm Hu},c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})
.
The second claim follows from the exact sequence:

0\to{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}H^{i-1}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})\to H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to\varprojlim_{n}H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})\to 0

and the vanishing of
{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\lim_{n}H^{i-1}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z})
that is implied by the first claim.
∎

Example 2.19
.

(Cohomology with compact support of the affine line
{\bf A}_{C}^{1}
).

Lemma 2.20
.

We have quasi-isomorphisms of solid
{\bf Z}_{\ell}
and
{\bf Z}_{p}
-modules, respectively:

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Z}_{\ell}(1))\simeq H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Z}_{\ell}(1))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Z}_{\ell}&\text{if }i=2,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(2.21)

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Z}_{p}(1))\simeq H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm naive}}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Z}_{p}(1))\simeq\begin{cases}({\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},\infty}/C)\oplus{\bf Z}_{p}&\text{if }i=2,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Recall that for all primes
\ell
(including
\ell=p
), we have

H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}({\bf A}_{C}^{1},{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}&\text{ if }i=0,\\
0&\text{ if }i\neq 0.\end{cases}

(2.22)

For
j\in{\bf N}
, consider the closed discs

D_{j}:=\{z\in{\bf A}^{1}_{C},|z|\leq p^{j}\}

and the associated distinguished triangle (
1
):

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}({\bf A}_{C}^{1},{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}({\bf A}_{C}^{1},{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to\operatorname{colim}_{j}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}({\bf A}_{C}^{1}\setminus D_{j},{\bf Z}_{\ell}).

Write
C_{j}:=\{z\in{\bf A}^{1}_{C},|z|\geq p^{j}\}
, then

\operatorname{colim}_{j}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}({\bf A}^{1}_{C}\setminus D_{j},{\bf Z}_{\ell}(1))\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{j}{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(C_{j},{\bf Z}_{\ell}(1)).

Consider
E_{j}:=\{z\in\mathbf{P}^{1},|z|\geq p^{j}\}
so that
C_{j}={\bf A}^{1}_{C}\cap E_{j}
. The Kummer sequence gives

H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(E_{j},{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}(1))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z}&\text{if }i=0,\\
C\{(p^{j}z)^{-1}\}^{*}/C\{(p^{j}z)^{-1}\}^{*\ell^{n}}&\text{if }i=1,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Using the Mayer-Vietoris sequence for
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{C}=E_{j}\cup{\bf A}^{1}_{C}
, we get

H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(C_{j},{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}(1))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}{\bf Z}&\text{if }i=0,\\
C\{(p^{j}z)^{-1}\}^{*}/C\{(p^{j}z)^{-1}\}^{*\ell^{n}}\oplus{\bf Z}/\ell^{n}&\text{if }i=1,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
\ell\neq p
then
C\{(p^{j}z)^{-1}\}^{*}
is
\ell
-divisible and we get:

H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(C_{j},{\bf Z}_{\ell}(1))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Z}_{\ell}&\text{if }i=0,\\
{\bf Z}_{\ell}&\text{if }i=1,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(C_{j},{\bf Z}_{p}(1))\simeq\begin{cases}{\bf Z}_{p}&\text{if }i=0,\\
C\{(p^{j}z)^{-1}\}^{*\wedge}\oplus{\bf Z}_{p}&\text{if }i=1,\\
0&\text{otherwise. }\end{cases}

Using (
2.22
) and the isomorphism

\operatorname{colim}_{j}C\{(p^{j}z)^{-1}\}^{*\wedge}\simeq({\mathscr{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},\infty}/C)

this yields
(
2.21
), as wanted.
∎

In particular, the above result recovers the groups
H^{2}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Z}_{\ell}(1))
and
H^{2}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Z}_{p}(1))
computed in
[
11
, Ex (A.2)]
in accordance with Theorem
2.8
.

For the rational cohomology, we will see later (see Example
7.3
) using syntomic techniques that we have:

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\simeq\begin{cases}0&\text{if }i\neq 2,\\
H^{1}_{c}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\mathscr{O}})\oplus{\bf Q}_{p}&\text{if }i=2.\end{cases}

Using the long exact sequence defining
H^{1}_{c}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\mathscr{O}})
and the vanishing of the coherent cohomology of
{\bf A}^{1}_{C}
, we obtain

H^{1}_{c}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\mathscr{O}})\simeq{\mathscr{O}}(\partial{\bf A}^{1}_{C})/{\mathscr{O}}({\bf A}^{1}_{C})
and

\displaystyle H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\simeq\begin{cases}0&\text{if }i\neq 2,\\
{\mathscr{O}}(\partial{\bf A}^{1}_{C})/{\mathscr{O}}({\bf A}^{1}_{C})\oplus{\bf Q}_{p}&\text{if }i=2.\end{cases}

In particular, we see that
H^{i}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Q}_{p}(1))\simeq H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Z}_{p}(1))\otimes^{\Box}_{{\bf Z}_{p}}{\bf Q}_{p}
for all
i
. As we will show in Theorem
2.23
this is a general phenomena.

2.2.3.
Comparison with the cohomology of Huber: rational coefficients

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 2.23
.

Let
X
be a partially proper rigid analytic variety over
L=K,C
. The quasi-isomorphism
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\xrightarrow{\sim}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})
from Theorem
2.8
induces a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}),

where we set
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}:={\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\otimes^{\scalebox{0.5}{$\square$}}_{{\bf Z}_{\ell}}{\bf Q}_{\ell}
.

We precede the proof with a handful of remarks. Thanks to Theorem
2.8
, we have a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c,{\rm Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}.

Therefore, Theorem
2.23
is equivalent to the following result, which has nothing to do with Huber’s compactly supported cohomology anymore.

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
X
be a partially proper rigid analytic variety over
L=K,C
. Then, the natural morphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Note that the same result is not true without compact support as the following basic example shows.

Example 2.25
.

Let
X={\bf A}^{1}_{C}
. Then the map

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}_{p})_{{\bf Q}_{p}}\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})

(2.26)

is not a quasi-isomorphism. Indeed, by
[
10
, Th. 1.8]
, the
H^{1}
of the target equals

H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{p})\simeq(\mathscr{O}(X)/C)(-1),

while the source
H^{1}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}_{p})
vanishes (e.g. by comparison with the cohomology of the scheme
H^{1}_{\text{\'{e}t}}({\bf A}^{1}_{C},{\bf Z}_{p})=0
).

In order to measure the failure of (
2.26
) being a quasi-isomorphism, let us introduce the following notation for its mapping fiber

P(X)=[{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})].

Naturally, a morphism
Y\to X
induces a map
P(X)\to P(Y)
.

Below we see rigid spaces as adic spaces and we use the language of adic geometry.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
X
be a rigid space and let
U\subseteq X
be a retrocompact open. Then the closure
\overline{U}
of
U
in
X
consists of all specializations of points of
U
. Moreover,
U
is equal to the interior of
\overline{U}
.

Proof.

This follows from a result in general topology
[
19
, Chapter 0, Cor. 2.2.27 and Prop. 2.4.4]
combined with the fact that the underlying topological spaces of rigid spaces (in Fujiwara–Kato’s sense) of type
V_{\mathbf{R}}
are reflexive valuative spaces
[
19
, Chapter II, Th. 8.2.19]
. Since the result is less difficult than having to move between different formalisms, we give a proof below.

Let
U^{\prime}
be the interior of
\overline{U}
in
X
. Trivially
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
and we need to show the reverse inclusion. The underlying topological space of
X
is a locally spectral space, so by
[
19
, Chapter 0, Cor. 2.2.27]
, the closure
\overline{U}
consists of all specializations of points of
U
inside
X
. Therefore
U
and
U^{\prime}
have the same closed points, in particular the same classical points.

Let
V\subseteq U^{\prime}
be an affinoid open. As
U\hookrightarrow X
is quasi-compact, the map
U\cap V\hookrightarrow V
is an open immersion of qcqs rigid spaces which is surjective on classical points. It follows that
U\cap V=V
, and since
V
was arbitrary, that
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 2.28
.

Let
X
be a qs rigid space, let
U,V\subseteq X
be quasi-compact opens such that
U\subset V
. Then
U\Subset V
if and only if there exists a partially proper
U^{\prime}
with
U\subseteq U^{\prime}\subseteq V
. Moreover, one can pick
U^{\prime}
so that
(X\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}
is a retrocompact open and
(X\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}\cup V=X
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{V}
be an open immersion of admissible formal schemes over
\mathscr{O}_{L}
which is a formal model of
U\subseteq V
, which exists because
U
and
V
are qcqs. The assumption that
U\Subset V
translates into the statement that
Z:=\overline{\mathfrak{U}_{0}}
(the closure of the special fiber of
\mathfrak{U}
inside the special fiber of
\mathfrak{V}
) is proper over
k
. Let
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
be the Berthelot tube of
Z
, i.e. the interior of its preimage
{\rm sp}^{-1}(Z)
under the specialization map
{\rm sp}\colon V\to\mathfrak{V}_{0}
. Then
U^{\prime}
is a partially proper open of
V
containing
U
by construction.

Lemma
2.27
above implies that the quasi-compact open
W={\rm sp}^{-1}(\mathfrak{V}_{0}\setminus Z)
is the interior of its closure, so that
{\rm sp}^{-1}(Z)
is the closure of its interior. This in turn implies that the interior
(V\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}
of
V\setminus U^{\prime}
equals
W
and hence is quasi-compact.

Finally, we claim that

(X\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}=(X\setminus V)\cup(V\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}.

Suppose we know this to hold, then
X\setminus V\subseteq(X\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}
implies that
X=V\cup(X\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}
. Moreover, we can check that
(X\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}
is retrocompact on the open cover
X=V\cup(X\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}
, and we are done.

It remains to show that if
x\in X\setminus V
belongs to the closure of
V
, then it lies in the closure of the quasi-compact open
W=(V\setminus U^{\prime})^{\circ}
. As taking the closure of a qc open amounts to adding specializations (Lemma
2.27
again), there exists a point
y\in V
specializing to
x
, and
x
belongs to the closure of
W
if and only if
y
does. But if
y
is not in the closure of
W
, it belongs to the interior of
X\setminus W
, which equals
U^{\prime}
. Since
U^{\prime}
is partially proper, it is closed under specialization in
X
(by the valuative criterion), and hence
x\in U^{\prime}
, a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 2.29
.

Let
X
be a partially proper rigid space. Then
X
can be written as the filtered colimit of opens
U\subseteq X
with the following properties:

(1)

U
is partially proper,

(2)

the closure of
U
in
X
is quasi-compact,

(3)

(X\setminus U)^{\circ}
is retrocompact in
X
.

Moreover, such a family is cofinal with the family of quasi-compact opens of
X
.

Proof.

Since
X
is partially proper, it can be written as the filtered colimit of a family of quasi-compact opens
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that for every
i\in I
there exists a
j\in I
with
j>i
such that
U_{i}\Subset U_{j}
. Pick such a
j
for every
i
. Applying Lemma
2.28
to
U_{i}\Subset U_{j}\subseteq X
, we obtain an open subset
U^{\prime}_{i}
with
U_{i}\subset U^{\prime}_{i}\subset U_{j}
which satisfies (1)–(3). It is easy to see that the resulting family of subsets (possibly no longer indexed by
I
) is cofiltering, and trivially covers
X
.
∎

Remark 2.30
.

The assertion of Corollary
2.29
is obvious in the Stein case. Embed
X\subseteq\mathbf{A}^{r}_{L}
as a closed analytic subspace, let
U_{n}=X\cap D(0,|p|^{-n})^{r}
be the intersection with a large closed polydisk, an affinoid open of
X
, and let
U^{\prime}_{n}=X\cap D^{\circ}(0,|p|^{-n})^{r}
be the intersection with the corresponding open polydisc, a pp open of
X
containing
U_{n-1}
. Then
(X\setminus U^{\prime}_{n})^{\circ}\cap U_{n}
is the product of circles
\{|x_{i}|=|p|^{-n}\}
, again an affinoid space.

Lemma 2.31
.

Let
X
be a rigid analytic variety over
L=K,C
.

(1)

If
X
is quasi-compact, then
P(X)=0
(i.e., (
2.26
) is a quasi-isomorphism).

(2)

If
X
is partially proper, then
P(X)\to P(\partial X)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Claim (1) is clear. For claim (2), write
X
as the filtering colimit of opens
U_{i}\subseteq X
(
i\in I
) as in Corollary
2.29
. Since the family is cofinal with the family of quasi-compact opens of
X
, we have

P(\partial X)=\varinjlim_{i\in I}\,P((X\setminus U_{i})^{\circ}).

It is therefore enough to show that the map
P(X)\to P((X\setminus U)^{\circ})
is an isomorphism for every
U
in the family. To this end, let
V\subseteq X
be a quasi-compact open containing
U
. Thus
X
can be covered by the quasi-compact open
V
and the non-quasi-compact open
(X\setminus U)^{\circ}
whose intersection
W
is quasi-compact by retrocompactness of
(X\setminus U)^{\circ}
. We have a morphism of distinguished triangles

\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}((X\setminus U)^{\circ},{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\oplus{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(V,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(W,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}((X\setminus U)^{\circ},{\bf Q}_{\ell})\oplus{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(V,{\bf Q}_{\ell})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(W,{\bf Q}_{\ell}).}

By (1), we have
P(V)=0=P(W)
, and it follows that
P(X)\to P((X\setminus U)^{\circ})
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.24
(and therefore also of Theorem
2.23
).

For every quasi-compact open
U\subset X
, let
U^{\prime}\subset U
denote its interior, which is a partially proper open. Since
X
is partially proper, the subsets
U^{\prime}
(as
U
varies over quasi-compact opens
U
) are cofinal with the subsets
U
(every
U
is contained in
V^{\prime}
for some
V
), so that we have

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})=\varinjlim_{U}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X\setminus U^{\prime},{\bf Q}_{\ell})

and, similarly,

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(\partial X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}=\varinjlim_{U}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X\setminus U^{\prime},{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}.

By Lemma
2.31
, the map
P(X)\to P(\partial X)
is a quasi-isomorphism. Therefore the square

\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})}

is cartesian.
Flipping the square, we deduce that
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})_{{\bf Q}_{\ell}}\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell})
is a quasi-isomorphism as well, as wanted.
∎

2.2.4.
Properties

We will now list the following properties of Huber’s étale cohomology with compact support (hence, in the case the spaces are smooth and partially proper, by Theorem
2.8
and Theorem
2.23
, of the pro-étale cohomology with compact support). All the spaces are adic.

(1)

If
X
is proper then we have a natural quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Z}_{\ell,\Box})

{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\simeq{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell}).

(2)

If
j:U\hookrightarrow X
is an open embedding then we have the pushforward map in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Z}_{\ell,\Box})

j_{*}:{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(U,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell}).

(3)

If
X
is quasi-compact, there is an exact sequence
[
23
, Cor. 2.4]
of solid
{\bf Z}_{\ell}
-modules

0\to{\mathrm{R}}^{1}\varprojlim_{n}H^{i-1}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})\to H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to\varprojlim_{n}H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})\to 0

(4)

Let
U
be a taut open subspace of
X
, let
Z=X\setminus U
, and let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion. Assume that
X,U
are partially proper. Then we have a distinguished triangle in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Z}_{\ell,\Box})

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(U,{\bf Z}_{\ell}|_{U_{n}})\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell})\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(Z,i^{*}{\mathrm{R}}\pi_{*}{\bf Z}_{\ell})

(5)

Let
{\mathbb{U}}
be an open covering of
X
such that every
U\in{\mathbb{U}}
is taut and, for every

U,V\in{\mathbb{U}}
, there exists a
W\in{\mathbb{U}}
such that
U\cup V\subset W
. Then, the map of solid
{\bf Z}_{\ell}
-modules

\varinjlim_{U\in{\mathbb{U}}}H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(U,{\bf Z}_{\ell}|_{U})\to H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(X,{\bf Z}_{\ell}),\quad i\geq 0,

is an isomorphism
[
23
, Prop. 2.1.]
.

(6)

Let
\{U\}_{i\in I}
be an admissible open covering of
X
by taut open varieties.
There is a spectral sequence
[
23
, Prop. 2.5.]

E_{1}^{a,b}=\bigoplus_{J\subset I,|J|=-a+1}H^{b}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(U_{J},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)|_{U_{J}})\Rightarrow H^{a+b}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},{\rm c,Hu}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

(2.32)

(7)

Let
X
be adic and partially proper and let
G
be a locally profinite group acting continuously on
X
.
Then
H^{i}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Z}/\ell^{n})
,
i\geq 0
, is a smooth
G
-module
[
3
, Cor. 7.8]
.

2.3.
Overconvergent setting

We define and study compactly supported
\ell
-adic pro-étale cohomology of smooth dagger varieties over
K
or
C
. As usual, we first give a local definition via presentations of dagger affinoids and then globalize it. We show that this definition coincides with the rigid analytic one for partially proper varieties.

2.3.1.
Definition

We define the overconvergent pro-étale cohomology with compact support by the following process: locally, if
X
is a dagger affinoid over
L=K,C
with a presentation
{\rm pres}(X)=\{X_{h}\}_{h\in{\bf N}}
of the dagger structure
6
6

6

See the Appendix of
[
38
]
for the definition and properties of presentations of dagger structures.
and rigid completion
\widehat{X}
,
we define in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))

\displaystyle:={\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial\{X\}_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)):=\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h}\setminus\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)),

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))

\displaystyle:={\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(\{X\}_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)):=[{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))],

where

{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)):={\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\{X\}_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)):=\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

Putting it together we get

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{h}[{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h}\setminus\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))]

(2.33)

\displaystyle=:\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{X}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

This form makes it clear that
{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))
is covariant for open embeddings. Indeed, let
U\to X
be an open embedding of smooth dagger affinoids. Choose compatible presentations
\{U_{h}\}
and
\{X_{h}\}
(i.e., so that the maps
U_{h}\to X_{h}
are also open embeddings). We define the pushforward map
{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(U,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))
via the composition

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{U}}(U_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{U}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)){\rightarrow}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{X}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

Here the first arrow is a quasi-isomorphism by the excision (
2.1
).

For a general smooth dagger variety
X
over
L
, we define the cosheaf
{\mathscr{A}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(r)
on
X
as the cosheaf
7
7

7

See
[
28
, Definition 5.5.4.1]
for a definition of a cosheaf in a
\infty
-category.
associated to the precosheaf defined by
U\mapsto{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(U,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)),U\in{\rm SmAff}^{\dagger}_{L}
,
U\to X
an open embedding. We set

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)):={\rm R}\Gamma(X,{\mathscr{A}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(r))\in{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box}).

(2.34)

If
L=K
, it is a dg
{\bf Q}_{\ell}
-algebra equipped with a continuous action of
{\mathscr{G}}_{K}
.

Lemma 2.35
.

Let
L=K,C
. Let
X
be in
{\rm Sm}_{L}^{\dagger}
.

(1)

(Local-global compatibility)
If
X
is a dagger affinoid then the natural map in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))

is a quasi-isomorphism.

(2)

(Passing to completion)
If
X
is partially proper then there is natural quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}({X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))

Proof.

For the first claim,
it suffices to show that for any open covering
X=X_{1}\cup X_{2}
by dagger affinoids, we have the Mayer-Vietoris distinguished triangle

{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\leftarrow{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{1},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\oplus{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\leftarrow{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X_{1}\cap X_{2},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

Choosing a presentation
\{X_{h}\}
of
X
, an
h\in{\bf N}
, and using excision we can write this triangle as

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{X}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\leftarrow{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{X}_{1}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\oplus{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{X}_{2}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\leftarrow{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{X_{1}\cap X_{2}}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)),

which is known to be distinguished.

Let us now prove claim (2).
If
X
is Stein, take
\{U_{n}\}
an increasing covering of
X
by admissible dagger affinoids. We have a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\widehat{X}\setminus\widehat{U}_{n},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\sim

\textstyle{\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus{U}_{n},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\sim

where the first row is the distinguished triangle (
1
) and the second one – the distinguished triangle (
2.40
). (We note that there is no circular reasoning here: the proof of Proposition
2.38
uses only the first claim of this lemma.) The second and third vertical arrows are quasi-isomorphisms by the usual comparison results between overconvergent cohomologies and their rigid analytic analogues (see
[
15
, Prop. 3.17]
). We obtain that the first vertical map (defined by the diagram) is a quasi-isomorphism as well.

Since a general partially proper variety
X
is locally Stein we can conclude by analytic codescent.
∎

Remark 2.36
.

(Comparison with the definition of Grosse-Klönne) Compactly supported de Rham cohomology of dagger varieties was defined by Grosse-Klönne in
[
21
]
and we can consider its pro-étale version. For a smooth dagger affinoid
X
over
L=K,C
,
set in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})
(see
[
21
, 4.3]
)

{\rm R}\Gamma^{\rm GK}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)):={\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{X}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)):=[{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\to{\mathrm{R}}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h}\setminus\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))],\quad h\in{\bf N}.

This is independent of
h
because of the excision (
2.1
).

Proposition 2.37
.

Let
X
be a smooth dagger affinoid.
There is a quasi-isomorphism in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})
:

{\rm R}\Gamma^{\rm GK}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

Proof.

This follows from the excision (
2.1
). Consider the following maps of distinguished triangles in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})

\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma^{\rm GK}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h_{0}},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h_{0}}\setminus\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\rm R}\Gamma^{\rm GK}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\wr}
\textstyle{\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h}\setminus\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{colim}_{h}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X_{h}\setminus\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).}

The existences of the first and the second distinguished triangle follow from the definition of
{\rm R}\Gamma^{\rm GK}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))
; of the last one from the definition of
{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))
.
The left vertical map is a quasi-isomorphisms by the excision (
2.1
). Also the dotted arrow exists and clearly is a quasi-isomorphism. This concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition 2.38
.

Let
X
be a smooth dagger Stein variety over
L=K,C
and let
\{U_{n}\}_{n}
be an admissible strict covering of
X
with dagger affinoids
U_{n}\Subset U_{n+1}
. Then there are natural quasi-isomorphisms in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})
:

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},U_{n}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},U_{n}}(U_{n+1},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

Remark 2.39
.

In particular, if we define the cohomology of the boundary of
X
as

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)):=\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus U_{n},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)),

we have a distinguished triangle:

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\partial X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

(2.40)

As in the rigid analytic set-up.

Proof.

Let
X
and
\{U_{n}\}
be as in the statement of the proposition. For each
n
, let
\{U_{n,h}\}_{h}
be a presentation of
U_{n}
. Then,

\displaystyle{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\xleftarrow{\sim}\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(U_{n},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\xleftarrow{\sim}\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\rm R}\Gamma^{\natural}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(U_{n},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\xleftarrow{\sim}\operatorname{colim}_{n}{\rm R}\Gamma^{\rm GK}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(U_{n},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))

where the first quasi-isomorphism comes from the fact that
{\mathscr{A}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}
is a cosheaf, the second one is the local-global compatibility from Lemma
2.35
, and the third one is the one from Proposition
2.37
.
For
h\in{\bf N}
, from the excision (
2.1
), we have

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}^{\rm GK}(U_{n},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))={\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{U}_{n}}(U_{n,h},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\xleftarrow{\sim}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},\widehat{U}_{n}}(\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

We conclude the proof using the quasi-isomorphisms
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\widehat{X},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\xleftarrow{\sim}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))
and
{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(\widehat{X}\setminus\widehat{U}_{n},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\xleftarrow{\sim}{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t}}(X\setminus U_{n},{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))
.
∎

2.3.2.
Mayer-Vietoris sequence

By definition, our compactly supported pro-étale cohomology satisfies a Mayer-Vietoris distinguished triangle:

Proposition 2.41
.

(Mayer-Vietoris sequence for dagger varieties.)

Let
X
be a dagger variety over
L=K,C
and let
X=U\cup V
an admissible open covering. Let
r\geq 0
. We have a distinguished triangle in
{\mathscr{D}}({\bf Q}_{\ell,\Box})
:

{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(U\cap V,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(U,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\oplus{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(V,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r))\to{\rm R}\Gamma_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(X,{\bf Q}_{\ell}(r)).

Proof.

The proposition follows from the formula (
2.34
) and the cosheaf condition for
{\mathscr{A}}_{\operatorname{pro\acute{e}t},c}(r)
.
∎

3.
Rigid analytic de Rham and Hyodo-Kato cohomologies with compact support

In this section we define compactly supported de Rham,
{\bf B}_{\rm dR}^{+}
-, and Hyodo-Kato cohomologies for smooth rigid analytic varieties over
K
or
C
. Our construction is inspired by the one of Chiarellotto in
[
8
]
(see also
[
36
]
,
[
4
]
). The usual
{\bf B}_{\rm dR}^{+}
- and Hyodo-Kato cohomologies we use here are the ones defined by Colmez-Nizioł in
[
15
]
,
[
16
]
.
